<meta>This is the title page for Jacob Boehme's work on the theological concept of predestination and grace, originally written in 1623 and printed in this edition in 1730.</meta>

## ON THE ELECTION OF GRACE, <note>original Latin: "DE ELECTIONE GRATIAE"</note>
->On the<-
# Election of Grace
->or<-
## On the Will of God over Mankind.
->That is,<-

**A brief explanation and introduction**
**to the highest ground <term>ground: "Grund" in German, referring here to the foundational principle or underlying reality</term>, how man may attain to Divine**
**Knowledge:**

**Also how the passages of Holy Scripture are to be un-**
**derstood, which deal with the fallen corrupted Adam,**
**and then with the new rebirth out of**
**Christ.**

### Written according to Divine Enlightenment
->by<-
## Jacob Boehme. <note>Jacob Boehme (1575–1624) was a German mystic and cobbler whose writings on the nature of God and evil had a profound influence on later philosophy and Romanticism.</note>
->in the year 1623; completed Feb. 8.<-

---

->Printed in the year of the brought-forth great Salvation<-
->1 7 3 0.<-

<summary>This title page introduces Jacob Boehme's theological treatise on predestination, the nature of the human will, and the process of spiritual rebirth. It frames the work as both an explanation of scripture and a guide to attaining direct divine knowledge.</summary>

**Preface of the Author to the Reader.** page 1

**Chapter 2.** Concerning the original state <term>original state: "Urstand" in the original German; Boehme uses this to describe the primordial condition of things before they entered time or physical form.</term> of God’s eternally-speaking Word, and the revelation of Divine Power, as Nature and Property. 12

**Chapter 3.** Concerning the introduction of the fiery science <term>science: "Scienz" in the original; for Boehme, this refers to a creative spark of attraction or a "root-knowing" that drives the manifestation of nature.</term> into the formation of Nature and Being; how this science introduces itself into fire, what that signifies, and how multiplicity arises. The Gate of the Great Mystery <note>original Latin: "Mysterium Magnum," a central concept in Boehme's work referring to the chaos or "mother" out of which all things are manifested.</note> of all secrets. 24

**Chapter 5.** Concerning the original state of Man. 50

**Chapter 6.** Concerning the Fall of Man and his Wife. 62

**Chapter 9.** Concerning the opposition of passages in the Scripture, as to the right understanding of the Scripture. 131

**Chapter 10.** A brief summary of the objections of the Scripture which hold human Reason captive; how they are to be understood. 167

**Chapter 11.** Further comparison and explanation of the passages concerning the Election. 183

**Chapter 12.** A brief report on several questions which lead Reason astray, wherein Reason thinks that God hardens man through a specific, predetermined will; how these are to be understood. 197

**Chapter 13.** A summary conclusion of all these questions. 217

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the actual text of Jacob Boehme's "De Electione Gratiae," following the table of contents. It introduces the primary conflict of the book: the tension between human reason’s view of predestination and the true nature of God's will. The catchword "seine" at the bottom of the page prepares the reader for the next page's opening sentence.</meta>

XV.
A
<meta>catchword: his</meta>

# ON THE ELECTION OF GRACE,
# Concerning the Election of Grace,
->or<-
## Concerning the Will of GOD over Humanity.
### The Author’s Preface to the Reader.
->Summaries.<-

Rational imagination regarding GOD falls into a false delusion concerning GOD’S counsel, §. 1. 2. as if GOD had chosen one person and rejected the other, and that everything must necessarily happen in such a way. 3. For while Holy Scripture indeed appears to say the same, it also conversely speaks much more of the contrary. 4. How, therefore, such a thing is to be understood in its fundamental ground? ibid. sq. <note>original Latin: "ibid. sq." (ibidem sequens), meaning "in the same place and following."</note>

When reason hears talk of GOD, regarding what He is according to His essence and His will, it imagines that GOD is something distant and foreign, who dwells outside the place of this world, high above the stars, and thus rules only through His spirit with an omnipresent power in the *location* <term>location: "Loco" in the original; referring to the spatial dimension or physical container of the universe.</term> of this world; but His majesty in the Trinity, where GOD is especially manifest, dwells in heaven outside the *location* of this world.

2. And therefore reason also falls into a creaturely delusion <note>creaturely delusion: Boehme uses this to describe the error of applying finite, human logic and the limitations of time/space to the infinite, eternal nature of God.</note>, as if GOD were something foreign, and had—before the times of the creation of creatures and this world—held a council within Himself in His Trinity through Wisdom, regarding what He wished to make and what the purpose of all being should be; and had thus conceived a purpose within Himself, toward which He wished to ordain every thing.

3. From which the contentious delusion has arisen regarding the counsel over humanity: as if GOD, out of His purpose, had chosen one part of humanity for the kingdom of heaven into His holy delight, and the other part for eternal damnation; in whom He wished to manifest His wrath, and conversely, upon the other elect, His grace. And thus, out of His purpose, He had made a distinction,

<vocab>Election of Grace, Counsel, Trinity, Reason, Kingdom of Heaven, The Elect, Damnation, God’s Will</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, Jacob Boehme challenges the traditional view of predestination, arguing that human reason wrongly perceives God as a distant ruler who arbitrarily decides the fate of individuals. He suggests that while Scripture may seem to support this "delusion," a deeper understanding of God's essence reveals a more complex truth.</summary>
<keywords>predestination, divine will, reason vs. faith, Trinity, election of grace, nature of God, salvation, damnation</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface of Jacob Boehme's "Concerning the Election of Grace." It completes the description of how human reason mistakenly views God’s decree of predestination as a rigid, dualistic system of love and wrath. Boehme then introduces his central metaphor of the tree to advocate for spiritual unity despite differing intellectual "gifts" or opinions.</meta>

...to let His power be seen in love and wrath; and therefore all things must necessarily happen in such a way, and the portion of wrath is so hardened and rejected by God's decree that no possibility for God's favor remains; whereas in the others, there is no possibility for damnation.

4. And although Holy Scripture speaks with almost identical sayings, and natural reason—which does not understand what God is—agrees with them, yet Scripture also conversely speaks much more of the *contrary* <term>contrary: "Contrarium" in the original Latin; refers to opposing or paradoxical viewpoints within the Bible</term>, that God wills nothing evil, nor has He made it by His decree. Regarding these two *contraries*—how this is to be properly understood in its fundamental ground—we wish to give the Christian impartial reader, the seekers and lovers of ground and truth, a brief hint for reflection (in order to unite them and establish a true understanding), and to present for their consideration our received gifts, as they have been grasped through the grace of the highest Good. Our intention is not to attack anyone or the opinions they have grasped, but to foster a Christian and brotherly union of our gifts, which we share through Divine grace.

5. Just as the branches and twigs of a tree do not look exactly alike in form, yet stand in one trunk, and one gives and introduces *essence* <term>essence: "Ens" in the original Latin; refers to the vital substance or "being" of a thing</term> and power to the other, and they all rejoice, bloom, and bear fruit in one trunk, and there is no resentment because of strength or inequality, and every branch works toward its own fruit and harvest; so it may also happen with our unequal gifts. If we only direct our desire into the right true mother <note>mother: In Boehme’s mystical thought, the "mother" represents the divine source or the "mysterium magnum" from which all life originates.</note>, as into our trunk, and if each branch of the tree always gives its power to the others in good will, and if we do not lead ourselves into a *selfhood* <term>selfhood: "Selbheit" in the original German; the state of ego-centricity or spiritual isolation from the whole</term> and the private pleasure of self-love, such as in *pride* <term>pride: "Hoffart" - an older German term for haughty arrogance</term> (wishing to rise in will above the mother in whom we stand and above all her children, wanting to be a tree of our own), nor take into ourselves the devil’s poison (of self-interest and false magnetic *impression* <term>impression: "Impreſſion" - the spiritual molding or "imprinting" of the soul by external or internal forces</term>), from which strife and ill-will, as well as schisms and divisions arise; where each branch of the human tree separates itself from the others, begrudging them their *essence* <term>essence: "Ens"</term> and power, and [viewing them] as apostate and

<meta>catchword: false</meta>

<summary>Boehme argues that while Scripture seems to support predestination, it also claims God wills no evil; he proposes to reconcile these "contraries" by viewing humanity as branches of a single divine tree that should share their unique spiritual gifts rather than succumb to prideful division.</summary>
<keywords>predestination, divine will, Ens, selfhood, spiritual unity, tree of humanity, pride, grace</keywords>

Boehme concludes his metaphor of the tree, explaining how religious divisions arise when individual "branches" (believers or sects) claim a monopoly on truth. He then transitions into Chapter 1, which explores the paradoxical nature of God as both a "Nothing" (beyond human understanding) and a "Something" (revealed through the Trinity).</meta>

Cap. 1. Election of Grace,
A 2
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

...proclaims falsely; but also shows and recognizes itself only as an apostate branch of its brothers in a false luster, from which the plurality of strife among humans has arisen.

6. To all these, we wish to indicate what the origin of strife is, and from where opinions and divisions naturally arise; also to indicate what the true ground of the One Religion is, from which so many opinions and divisions have emerged, and how the *contrary* <term>contrary: "Contrarium" in the original; refers to the opposing forces of love and wrath that seem to contradict God's unity</term> of the world arose, for a greater understanding of the Divine Will, according to love and wrath, and how all this is to be fundamentally understood.

7. And I admonish the beloved reader to sink himself in Divine humility into God and his fellow-branches or brothers; thus he may well grasp our received deep sense and concept, and be turned from all errors into the true rest (where all things rest within the Word and Power of God): And we commend him to the working love in the *Being of Christ* <term>Being of Christ: "Ente Christi" in the original; from the Latin *ens*, referring to the spiritual substance or essence of Christ</term>, and our well-disposed will and desire into His will, Amen.

->The 1st Chapter.<-

# Concerning the single Will of God, and concerning the introduction of His being of His revelation. What the one God is?

->Summaries.<-

God is a Single God. §. 1. How He can be in wrath and love, since He is a "Nothing," without will and affects? 2. 3. He is a Single Will, which is ungraspable, 4. and generates within Himself a graspable will for His revelation. 5. The first ungraspable <note>original: "unfaßliche"</note> will is called Father; the graspable, Son; the outflow, Spirit; and that which has flowed out, Wisdom. 6. This threefold essence is a life and knowledge incomprehensible to all creatures, 7. like the sun in nature. 8. Thus the Single God leads Himself out in Trinity. 9. The graspability is His heart or mind. 10. The Father generates Himself out, 11. and is only One God of threefold operation, and is called Spirit with His outflowing; 12. the Son is the Father’s power; and the Spirit the life; 13. the 4th operation is Wisdom, 14. in which Divine *imagination* <term>imagination: "Imagination" in Boehme's theology is the creative, "imaging" power of God that brings spiritual forms into being</term> the Angelic and Soul-like image of God is understood. 15. Outside nature and creature, however, God is One, whose name is JEHOVAH. 16. This *imagination* has no beginning, except in the creation, and is in itself infinite, like...

<summary>Boehme attributes human religious conflict to a lack of understanding regarding God's unified nature and introduces his metaphysical system where God, though a "Nothing," reveals Himself through the Trinity and Divine Wisdom.</summary>

XV. Concerning Cap. 1.
<meta>catchword: beget-</meta>

the shaping of the human mind; which however in the earthly creature mostly originates from the **fantasy of the stars** <note>original: "Phantasey des Gestirns"; Boehme refers to the belief that the "starry spirit" or cosmic influences shape human psychology and worldly desires.</note>.
17. This One God has only One Will, to give and beget Himself. 18. As the sun is in nature: so God outside of nature is the Only Good, from which no evil comes, 19. 20. and has nothing that He can love but Himself; is neither angry nor merciful. 21. The Father loves His Son as His own revelation, just as the soul loves the body; 22. the Son desires the Father as His life, and is the Father’s delight; 23. the Holy Spirit is the worker and leader in that delight, as in wisdom. 24. In God there is no will toward evil, otherwise He would be at odds with Himself and divided; 25. 26. He has no locality, for He is Himself Everything. 27. Objection and its answer. 28. 29. God shall be sought alone within the human being. ibid. The nearest way to Him. ibid. From where do good and evil originate? 30. 31.

---

God speaks in Moses, in a revealed voice to the people of Israel: (under which voice He introduced and let Himself be heard from His hiddenness into a revealed sound, in a formal creaturely way, so that the creature might grasp Him:)
> I the Lord your God, am one God, you shall honor no other gods beside me.

Exod. 20: 2. 3. Deut. 6: 4. also Moses says:
> The Lord our God is an angry, jealous God, and a consuming fire.

Also in another place:
> God is a merc<unclear>if</unclear>ul God.

Also, His Spirit is a flame of love. (Deut. 4: 24-31.)

2. These just-recounted sayings seem to be an entirely *contrary* <note>original: "Contrarium"; an opposing or contradictory state.</note> matter, in that God calls Himself an angry God and a consuming fire: and then also a flame of love, which can be nothing but good alone—otherwise He would not be God, as the only Good.

3. For one cannot say of God that He is this or that, evil or good, or that He has distinctions within Himself: For He is in Himself **nature-less**, as well as **affect-less** and **creature-less**. He has no inclination toward anything, for there is nothing before Him to which He could incline, neither evil nor good: He is in Himself the **Abyss** <term>Abyss: "Ungrund" in the original; Boehme’s term for the "groundless" or "bottomless" nothingness out of which God and all existence emerge.</term>, without any will toward nature and the creature, as an eternal Nothing; there is no **striving quality** <note>original: "Qual"; a play on words in 17th-century German meaning both "torment" and "source-quality" (Quelle).</note> in Him, nor anything that could incline toward Him or away from Him. He is the Single Being, and there is nothing before Him or after Him, by which or in which He could create or conceive a will for Himself; He also has nothing that could 
beget-

<summary>Boehme addresses the apparent contradictions in Scripture regarding God's nature as both a consuming fire and a merciful love. He explains that in His purest essence, God is the "Unground"—a nature-less, desire-less void beyond all human categories of good and evil.</summary>
<keywords>Unity of God, Trinity, Unground (Ungrund), nature-less essence, wrath vs. love, Mosaic law, fantasy of the stars</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the main text of Chapter 1, moving from the introduction into the core of Jacob Boehme's cosmogony. It defines the nature of the Trinity and the "Ungrund" (Abyss) as the starting point of all existence.</meta>

# Cap. 1. The Election of Grace

begets or gives: He is the Nothing and the All, and is a Single Will, in which the world and the whole creation lie; in Him all is equally-eternal without beginning, in equal weight, measure, and goal; He is neither light nor darkness, neither love nor wrath, but the Eternal One; therefore Moses says: *The Lord is one God.* (Deut. 6: 4.)

4. This same bottomless <note>original: *ungründliche*; referring to the *Ungrund* or the groundless abyss of the divine essence before any manifestation</note>, inconceivable, unnatural, and un-creaturely Will, which is only One, and has nothing before it nor behind it; which in itself is only One, which is as a Nothing and yet is All: That is and is called the One God; who conceives and finds Himself in Himself, and begets God out of God.

5. Namely: The first unbeginning Single Will, which is neither evil nor good, begets in itself the Single eternal Good, as a conceivable will, which is the Son of the bottomless Will, and yet is co-eternal within the unbeginning Will; and this same second will is the first will’s eternal sensitivity and findability, wherein the Nothing finds itself as "Something" within itself: and the Unfindable, as the bottomless Will, goes out through its eternally Found [Will], and leads itself into an eternal contemplation of itself.

6. Thus (1) the bottomless Will is called **Eternal Father**; (2) and the found, conceived, born Will of the Abyss is called his **born or Only-Begotten Son**, for he is the Abyss's *Essence* <term>Ens: a Latin term Boehme uses to describe the fundamental spiritual substance or "being-ness" of a thing</term>, wherein the Abyss conceives itself into a Ground. (3) And the proceeding <note>original: *Ausgang*</note> of the bottomless Will, through the conceived Son or Essence, is called **Spirit**, for He leads the conceived Essence out of Himself into a weaving or living of the Will, as a life of the Father and the Son: (4) and that which has proceeded is the **Delight**, as the Found [state] of the eternal Nothing, wherein the Father, Son, and Spirit see and find themselves; and it is called **God’s Wisdom** or **Contemplation**.

7. This triple Being in its birth, in its self-contemplation of Wisdom, has ever been from eternity, and possesses in itself no other ground nor place but itself; it is a single Life, and a single Will without desire, and is neither thick nor thin, neither high nor deep; it is no space, time, nor place, nor does it possess in itself thickness or thinness, height or depth, space or time, but is through all in all, and yet to the All is as an inconceivable Nothing.

<meta>8. Just as [Gleich]</meta>

<summary>Boehme describes the "Ungrund" or the bottomless abyss of God as a unified Will that exists before all things, eventually manifesting as the Trinity (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit) through a process of self-finding and divine contemplation.</summary>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 1 of the "Election of Grace," expanding on the nature of the divine essence before the creation of the world. It builds on the previous page's definition of the Trinity by using the analogy of sunlight and explaining how the "Abyss" (Ungrund) becomes a "Ground."</meta>

# Concerning Chapter 1.

8. Just as the sun's brilliance works in the whole world, in everything and through everything, and yet the "All" <note>Referring to the totality of created things</note> can take nothing from the sun but must simply endure it and work through the sun's power: in such a way is God to be considered—what He is outside of nature and creation in Himself, in a self-comprehending Chaos <note>In the 17th-century context, "Chaos" refers to an infinite, formless void or "gaping hole," not necessarily disorder</note>, outside of ground, time, and place. There, the eternal Nothingness conceives itself into an "Eye," or an eternal Seeing, for its own self-contemplation, sensitivity, and findability. In this state, one cannot say that God has two wills, such as one for evil and the other for good.

9. For in the un-natural, un-creaturely Godhead there is nothing more than a single Will, which is also called the One God. He wills nothing more within Himself than to find and grasp Himself, to go out from Himself, and through that "going out" to lead Himself into a state of self-contemplation. In this, one understands the Trinity of the Godhead along with the mirror of His Wisdom as the Eye of His seeing. Within this mirror, all powers, colors, wonders, and beings are understood in the eternal (single) Wisdom in equal weight and measure, without properties <term>Properties: Boehme uses "Eigenchaften" to mean specific qualities or driving forces (like heat, cold, or bitterness) that define individual existence</term>, as a single ground of the Essence of all Essences. It is a joy found within itself, or a desire for "Something"—a desire for the revelation or discovery of properties. Yet this Divine Joy or Wisdom within itself, in the first ground, is still entirely without properties. For if there were properties, there would also have to be something that gave and caused those properties. But there is no cause for the Divine powers, nor for the Divine Joy or Wisdom, other than simply the single Will—namely, the One God—who leads Himself into a Trinity, as into a graspability of Himself. (This graspability is the Center <note>original: *Centrum*</note>, as the eternal, grasped "One.") This is called the Heart or Seat of the eternal Will of God, where the Abyss <note>original: *Ungrund*; the bottomless, foundational void of God</note> possesses itself in a "Ground." This is the single "place" of God, and yet it exists without any division or separateness; it is also entirely immeasurable, without any form or likeness, for there is nothing before it to which it could be compared.

10. This Heart or Center of the Abyss is the eternal Mind, as well as the Willing; and yet it has nothing before it that it can will, except only the single Will which conceives itself into this Center. Thus, the first Will also has the Center as its focus.

<meta>catchword: also</meta>

<summary>Boehme uses the analogy of sunlight to explain how God exists as a single, property-less Will within the "Ungrund" (Abyss) before manifesting as the Trinity. He describes the divine "Wisdom" as a mirror through which the formless Nothingness begins to contemplate itself as a "Something."</summary>

<keywords>Ungrund, Abyss, Trinity, Divine Wisdom, One Will, Self-contemplation, Center, Properties</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the divine nature before creation. It specifically details how the "Abyss" (Ungrund) or groundless Will of God manifests as the Trinity: the Father (the Will), the Son (the "Place" or Heart), and the Holy Spirit (the outgoing breath or power).</meta>

# Chapter 1: The Election of Grace

...nor anything that he could want, except for this single place of his self-findability: Thus the first Will is the Father of his Heart, or the place of his finding, and a possessor of what is found, as his inborn Will or Son.

11. The groundless Will, which is the Father and the beginning of all being, begets within itself a place of graspability <note>Böhme uses "graspability" (Faßlichkeit) to describe how the infinite void takes on a conceptual form or "container" so it can be known</note>; or he possesses the place: and the place is the ground and beginning of all beings, and in turn possesses the groundless Will, which is the Father of the beginning as the ground.

12. Thus the Father and his Son (as the place of a selfhood) are a single GOD, of a single Will; which single Will, in the grasped place of the ground, goes out from itself from the grasp, where He is called a Spirit with the going out: and the Single Will of the Abyss <term>Abyss: Translated from the German "Ungrund," referring to the "groundless" void of God before any form or nature existed</term> separates, with the first, eternal, beginningless grasp, into a threefold working and yet remains only One Will. As the first Will, which is called Father, works within itself the Son, as the place of the Godhead: and the place of the Godhead, which is the Father’s Son, works within itself in findability, as the power of Wisdom; which powers all originate in the Son, and here all powers are yet only a single power, and that is the sensitive, findable Godhead in itself, in a single Will and Being, without any distinction.

13. The first Will (which is called Father) breathes out these found, born, and worked powers—as the Center of the beginnings of all beings—in the sensitivity of itself, out of the single power which is his seat or Son: In the same way as the sun’s rays shoot out from the magical fire of the sun, and reveal the sun’s power; so that same outgoing is a beam of the power of GOD, as a moving life of the Godhead, where the groundless Will has introduced itself into a ground, as into a surging power: the Will breathes the same out into power from the power, and the outgoing is called the Spirit of GOD, and makes the Third Working, as a life or weaving in the power.

14. The Fourth Working now happens in the breathed-out power, as in the Divine Contemplation or Wisdom, where the Spirit of GOD (which originates from the power) with the breathed-out powers, as with a single power with

<summary>This page explains the internal dynamics of the Trinity, describing God as a "groundless Will" that creates a "place" (the Son) to find itself, and then breathes out power (the Spirit). Böhme uses the analogy of sunlight to describe how the divine essence manifests its hidden powers as "Wisdom."</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Father, Son, Holy Spirit, Abyss, Ungrund, Wisdom, Divine Power, self-findability, graspability</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine nature before the physical world existed. On the previous page, Böhme explained how the groundless Will (the Father) generates the Heart (the Son) and the Spirit. Here, he describes how this internal divine process acts as a "pre-model" for the eventual creation of angels and humanity.</meta>

# XV. On the [Election of Grace] Chapter 1.

[God] plays with Himself, as He introduces Himself into the power of the formations in the Divine Delight, as if He wished to introduce an image of this birth of the Trinity into a specific will and life, as a pre-modeling of the singular Trinity. And this very modeled image is the delight of Divine Contemplation <note>German: *Beschaulichkeit*. In Böhme's mystical philosophy, this refers to the state where the internal, invisible God becomes visible or "contemplatable" to Himself</note>; and yet one should not understand it as a tangible, creaturely image of a limited form, but rather as the Divine Imagination, as the first ground of Magic <term>Magic: From the German *Magiæ*. For Böhme, "Magic" is the divine creative power that brings "something" out of "nothing"—the spiritual process of manifestation</term> from which Creation took its beginning and original state.

15. Also, in this same internal modeling, or magical grasping within Wisdom, the true Angelic and Soul-like image of God is understood, as Moses says: (Genesis 1:27) "God created man in his image," that is, in the image of this Divine internal modeling according to the spirit; and He created him in the image of God according to the creature of the created physical form. The same is to be understood regarding the Angels, according to the Divine Essence out of Divine Wisdom. However, the creaturely ground—in which the properties lie—shall be indicated hereafter.

16. In this aforementioned account, we now understand in a brief summary what God is outside of Nature and Creature, as He says in Moses: "I the Lord your God am one God." His name is called in the sensual tongue <note>The "sensual tongue" refers to the "Language of Nature," where the sound and vibration of words reflect the inner essence of the thing named</note> (where this Divine birth introduces itself within the powers of the singular Wisdom into a grasping of the image of itself) **JEHOVA**, as a grasped delight of the Nothing into Something, or the eternal One, which might perhaps be sketched in a way with such a <image-desc>triangle with a dot in the center representing the Trinity</image-desc> formation; and yet it is not a measurable or divisible image or being, but rather something for the mind to ponder.

17. For this internal formation within itself is neither great nor small, and has no beginning nor end anywhere, except only where the Divine Delight introduces itself into a being for its contemplation, as in the Creation; within itself, however, the formation is infinite, and the shaping is uncircumscribed. Just as the internal modeling of the human mind stands immeasurable in an everlasting form, where countless many thoughts may model and grasp themselves in the single mind; in the earthly creature, however, these mostly originate from the fantasy of the star-mind <term>Star-mind: Also called the "astral" or "sidereal" mind. Böhme believed human thoughts are often influenced by the celestial rotations and the physical world rather than the pure divine source</term> and not from the powers of the inner ground of Divine Wisdom.

<meta>catchword: 18. Al-</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains the "Divine Imagination" as a pre-creation state where God models the Trinity into a "delight" that eventually serves as the blueprint for angels and humanity. He defines the name JEHOVA as the manifestation of the Eternal One moving from "nothing" into "something."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of God's internal nature before the existence of the material world. The previous page established that God "modeled" the human soul and the angelic form within His own Wisdom before time began. Here, Böhme explains how God's internal will functions as a pure, self-generating unity of love, using the metaphor of light and the sun to explain how God is "nothing" to the physical world while being "everything" in His own essence.</meta>

### Chapter 1. The Election of Grace

18. At this point, we wish to remind the reader that God in Himself—insofar as He is called "God" outside of nature and created things—has no more than one single will, which is: that He gives and begets Himself. God **JEHOVAH** <note>original: *JEHOVA*. A traditional name for God used here to emphasize His eternal, self-existing nature</note> begets nothing but God; that is, there begets itself only one Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, into the one Divine Power and Wisdom.

19. Just as the sun has only one single will, which is to give itself and to push outward with its desire into all things—to grow, to give power to all life, and to pour itself into everything—so too, in the same way, God outside of nature and creation is the only Good, who neither can nor will give anything except God, or the Good.

20. Outside of nature, He is the greatest gentleness and humility, wherein no will toward either good or evil inclination is felt, for there is neither evil nor good before Him. He is Himself the Eternal One Good, and the beginning of all good essence and will. It is also impossible for anything evil to penetrate into Him, insofar as He is that same One Good; for to all things that come after Him, He is a "Nothing" <note>For Böhme, God is "Nothing" (*Nichts*) not because He doesn't exist, but because He is "No-Thing"—He precedes all physical matter, definitions, and limitations</note>. He is a spiritual power, active and essential within Himself; the highest and simplest humility and well-doing; like the feeling of love, or the tasting of love and goodness; in the sense of the sweet "birthing" <note>German: *Gebärung*. Böhme often uses biological terms like "birthing" or "generating" to describe how God's attributes constantly emerge from one another</note>, or the joyful hearing of it.

21. For all the senses function in equal harmony, and there is nothing but a lovely welling up of the Holy Spirit in the One Wisdom. There, one cannot speak of an "Angry God," nor even of a "Merciful God," for in this state there is no cause for anger, nor any cause to "choose" to love something; for He is the One Love itself, who leads and begets Himself into pure love within the Trinity.

22. The first will, which is called the **Father**, loves His **Son** as the "Heart" of His own self-revelation, because the Son is His <term>Perceptibility: From the German *Findlichkeit*. This refers to the state where the "unground" or hidden God becomes "findable" or manifest to Himself</term> and power. Just as the soul loves the body, so in the same way, the grasped will of the Father is His power and spiritual body—the center of the Godhead, or of the Divine "Something," wherein the first will becomes a "Something."

23. And the Son is the humility of the first will (the Father) and in turn powerfully desires the Father's will; for without the Father, He would be a nothing, and He becomes...

<meta>catchword: right</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains that God's internal nature is a singular, self-generating will of pure love and humility, comparing God's radiance to the sun. He defines the relationship between the Father and Son as the process by which the divine "No-thing" becomes a manifest "Something."</summary>

...rightly called the Father’s pleasure, or the desire for the revelation of powers, such as the Father’s taste, smell, hearing, feeling, and seeing. And yet, one should not understand these as distinct divisions here, for all these senses <term>senses: original Latin *Sensus*</term> lie in equal weight within the Single Godhead; rather, think only that these senses, which originate in the ground of nature, arise in the way that the Father speaks these powers out of Himself into a distinct manifestation.

24. And the Holy Spirit <term>Holy Spirit: original German *H. Geist*</term> is called Holy, and a flame of love, because He is the outgoing power from the Father and the Son; He is the moving life in the first will of the Father and in the second will of the Son in His power, and He is a shaper, worker, and guide in the outgoing pleasure of the Father and the Son (that is, within the Wisdom).

25. Therefore, dear brothers—you poor people confused by Babel <term>Babel: A term Böhme uses to describe the "confusion" of competing church dogmas and sectarian fighting, likening it to the Tower of Babel</term>, who have been confounded by the envy of Satan—take note of this: When you are told of three persons of the Godhead, and of the Divine Will, know that the Lord our God is one single God, who neither can nor will desire anything evil. For if He willed something evil and then also something good within Himself, there would be a division in Him, and there would have to be something else that was the cause of a contrary <term>contrary: original Latin *Contrarii*, meaning an opposing force or quality</term>.

26. Since nothing exists before God, nothing can move Him toward anything. For if something moved Him, that thing would be earlier and greater than He Himself, and it might happen that God was at odds with Himself and divided; thus, that moving thing would also have to have a beginning, since it moved.

27. But we tell you in the testimony of the One: that God’s essence (insofar as it is called the One God) should be understood as existing without ground <note>original: *ausser Grund*. This refers to Böhme's concept of the *Ungrund*—the absolute, foundationless "abyss" or "void" that precedes all existence</note>, place, or time, dwelling within itself; it is not to be viewed as being in any one specific location with a particular home. But if you wish to know where God dwells, then take away nature <term>nature: original Latin *Natur*</term> and creature <term>creature: original Latin *Creatur*</term>, and then God is everything. Take away the spoken, formed Word, and you see the eternally-speaking Word that the Father speaks in the Son, and you see the hidden wisdom of God.

28. But you say: "I cannot take nature and creature away from me, for if that happened, I would be a..."

<summary>Böhme explains that God is a unified, non-dual being whose "senses" exist in perfect balance before being spoken into existence. He warns that the "confusion of Babel" (religious dogma) obscures the truth that God is the source of only good and exists beyond the limitations of time, place, and material nature.</summary>

# Chapter 1. The Election of Grace.

...nothing; therefore I must model the Godhead for myself through images, because I see that there is both evil and good within me, just as it is understood to be within the whole of creation.

29. Listen, my brother: God spoke through Moses: "You shall not make for yourself any image of any God, whether in heaven, on earth, in the water, or in anything." This was to indicate that He is not an image, nor does He require any location for a seat <note>Böhme emphasizes that God does not sit on a physical throne in a specific place, but is an omnipresent spiritual reality.</note>, and one should not seek Him in any specific place, but only in His formed, spoken Word—as the image of God within the human being themselves, as it is written: *The word is near you, even in your mouth and in your heart.* (Romans 10:8). And that is the nearest way to God: that the image of God within itself sinks away from all modeled images, abandons all mental pictures, disputes, and strife within itself, despairs of its own personal will, desire, and opinion, and sinks itself purely and alone into the Eternal One—into the clear, single love of God, which He reintroduced into humanity through Christ after the Fall.

30. I have presented this in a somewhat detailed way so that the reader may learn to understand the first ground <term>ground: original German *Grund*, meaning the foundational essence or "bottom" of a concept</term> of what God is and what He wills. I wish for the reader not to look for a "good" or "evil" will within the Single, un-natural <note>original: *unnatürlichen*. Here meaning "existing before or beyond the laws of nature."</note>, un-creaturely God, and that he should move away from physical images of the creature when he wishes to contemplate God, His will, and His eternally-speaking Word. And when he wishes to contemplate from where "Evil" and "Good" originate—because of which God calls Himself an angry, jealous God—let him turn his mind toward **Eternal Nature**, as the spoken, compacted, and formed Word, and then toward nature itself as the beginning, temporal nature in which the creation of this world lies.

31. So we now wish to further inform the reader regarding God’s Word (which He speaks out of His own powers), and point out to him the division, namely:
(1) The origin of the properties from which a good and evil will arises;
(2) To what end such a thing must be inevitable;
(3) How all things stand within this inevitability;
(4) And how malice arises within the creature.

XV. Regarding Chapter 2.

## The Second Chapter.

## Concerning the Primordial Origin <term>primordial origin: original German *Urstand*, referring to the foundational or pre-existent state of a thing</term> of God's Ever-Speaking Word, and the Manifestation of Divine Power as Nature and Property.

### Summaries.

1. Because the created being is reliant on images, it creates a pictorial image of God and thinks of Him in terms of mental pictures. 2. If there were a formal decree or resolution within God, He would have to have a beginning <note>Böhme argues that God's nature is an eternal "now" rather than a series of decisions made over time.</note>. 3. Now, however, He has nothing before Him, and is Himself All. 4. He only desires to beget His Son, and cannot be at odds with His own self. 5. Likewise, we should think of those things that proceed from the eternal ground, whose will is unchangeable unless they break themselves off from the whole into their own self-will <term>self-will: original German *Eigenes*, meaning a state of being "own" or separate from the divine unity</term>. 6. From God's powers, angels and humans were made (as can be seen in John 1:1-3). 7. Everything originates in the Word. 8. The Will, as God the Father, is the beginning, which conceives itself into a desire. 9. 10. Thus, two kinds of powers are to be understood. 11. The evil will is a devil. 12. 13. The powers that lead to the Word are God, and the "magnetic pulling" is the beginning of nature, 14. wherein the foundation of darkness is understood, in which God calls Himself an "angry God" <note>In Böhme’s thought, "anger" or "wrath" is a technical term for the fiery, foundational energy of nature when it is not tempered by the light of Love.</note>. Ibid. The example of a burning candle. 15. Without nature, God would be still; but nature leads itself into laboring anguish <term>laboring anguish: original German *Peinlichkeit*, referring to the tension and friction of the natural forces required to produce life</term>, and serves as the tool of the still eternity. 16. 17. Just as all properties lie within a single essence in a candle, so too all properties lie hidden in the One, hidden God. 19. 20. In His indrawing desire, nature originates, as does the essence known as the *Mysterium Magnum* <note>original: "Great Mystery," the chaotic potential out of which God creates all things</note>. God's manifestation involves light and darkness, and the choice [between them]; 21. and in nature, one thing is set against the other to provide the movement and manifestation of God; hence, the creation. 22. The example of the sun, 23. which unlocks the mystery of the outer world and exists one degree deeper in nature than the mystery of the outer world, 24. but it places its desire into the mystery, specifically into Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury <note>Böhme uses these alchemical terms to describe the three fundamental qualities of all matter: contraction (Salt), expansion/fire (Sulfur), and movement/spirit (Mercury).</note>; 25. just as these, in turn, place their desire in the sun, ibid. as the stars also do, taking their radiance from the power of the sun. 26. Thus, God has spoken a likeness of His essence into a period of time. 27. Without Eternal Nature, however, He would not be manifest, 28. as can be perceived through the relationship of fire and light. 29. Therein lies the great *Arcanum* <note>original: "Secret" or "Hidden Mystery"</note> of the spiritual essence. 30. The eternal Good becomes full of joy through the laboring anguish, 31. for the life of love originates from that very anguish. 32. By the fire, "eternal nature" is understood, in which God is a consuming fire and is angry. 33. 34. According to the light, however, He is called God alone, in whom there is only a single will of love. 35. The example of the earth, from which flowers grow alongside thistles and thorns. 36. In God, there is no will toward evil, nor any knowledge of evil. 37. Every creature, however, has the "fire-sting" <term>fire-sting: original German *Feuer-Angel*, meaning the core spark of desire or hunger that drives a living being</term> within itself, 38. yet it should [sink itself] into the light's temperature <term>light's temperature: original German *Lichts-Temperatur*, meaning the perfect balance and harmony of the light</term>...

Natural reason resides in the formed, grasped, spoken Word; therefore, it is a pictorial <term>pictorial: original German *bildlich*, meaning based on images, mental representations, or metaphors rather than the direct, unmediated reality of God</term> being, and it constantly thinks that GOD is also a pictorial being who might become angry and lead Himself into properties of both evil and good. To that extent, reason has imagined for itself, concerning this high article of the Divine Will, that GOD from eternity made a purpose and a choice for Himself regarding what He wanted to do with His creatures; and that He thus led Himself into a state of vengeance so that He could and might reveal His love and mercy upon His chosen ones; and that His grimness must therefore be a cause for His mercy to be recognized. All of this is fundamentally true in the sense that God’s wrath must reveal His majesty, just as the fire reveals the light.

2. But regarding the will of GOD, as well as the distinctiveness of the formed word and the creature, reason has no correct concept. For if GOD had ever held a council <term>council: original German *Rath*, implying a deliberative meeting, a plan, or a legislative decree</term> within Himself to reveal Himself in such a way, then His revelation would not be from eternity, outside of mind and place; likewise, that same council would have to have had a beginning at some point, and there would have to have been a cause within the Godhead for the sake of which GOD in His Trinity would have deliberated. There would also have to be thoughts in GOD which modeled <note>original: *einmodelte*, implying the shaping of a form like a sculptor with a mold</note> things for Him in the way He wished to encounter a thing.

3. But now He Himself is the One, and the ground of all things, and the eye of all beings, and the cause of all essence: nature and creature arise out of His property. Why then would He deliberate with Himself, since there is no enemy before Him nor behind Him, and He alone is everything Himself—the willing, the doing, and the power?

4. Therefore, if we wish to speak only of GOD's unchangeable essence—what He wills, what He has willed, and always wills—we should not speak of His council, for there is no council in Him; He is the eye of all seeing, and the ground of all beings. He wills and does only one thing within Himself forever: He begets Himself as Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, into the wisdom of His revelation. Otherwise, the One, unfathomable <term>unfathomable: original German *ungründliche*, referring to the *Ungrund*, the "groundless" or "bottomless" void from which all existence emerges</term> GOD wills nothing in Himself, nor has He in Himself any

<summary>Böhme argues that human reason incorrectly projects a "human-like" decision-making process onto God, whereas God’s will is actually an eternal, spontaneous self-revelation. He critiques the idea of a literal "divine council" or predestination decree, explaining that God is a singular, eternal essence without an external opponent to deliberate against.</summary>

For if He desired more within Himself, He would have to be not omnipotent enough in His willing to accomplish such a thing; thus, in Himself, He can do nothing more than will only Himself: for whatever He has willed from eternity, that He is Himself. Therefore He is alone One, and nothing more: thus no single thing can become contentious with Him, from which a council might arise to decide the disputes.

5. Thus we should also think regarding those things which proceed from the eternal, beginningless ground, that every thing which is out of the eternal ground is a thing in its selfhood <term>selfhood: original German *Selbheit*, referring to the individual identity or autonomous existence of a created being</term>, and is its own will, which has nothing before it that may break it; unless it leads itself into a foreign form <term>form: original German *Fassung*, implying a conceptual frame, a container, or a way of "grasping" reality</term>, which does not resemble the first ground from which it arose; if so, it is a separation from the Whole. As we are to understand regarding the fallen devil and the soul of man, that the creature broke itself away from the whole will, and introduced itself into a self-interest of a different form (contrary to the Divine single birth). To understand this, however, we must look to the primary cause of how that could have happened.

6. For if the powers of the single Divine property had not introduced themselves into divisibility <term>divisibility: original German *Schiedlichkeit*, the state of being separated, differentiated, or "distinguishable" from a unified whole</term>, then that could not have been, and there would have been neither angel nor other creature; nor would there be nature or property, and the invisible GOD would be manifest only to Himself in the quiet, working wisdom within Himself. All beings would be one single being—though one could not even speak of "beings," but rather of a self-working delight <term>delight: original German *Lust*, here meaning a joyous impulse or the "will to become" that exists before any physical thing is made</term>, which indeed exists only in the One GOD, and nothing more.

7. But when we contemplate the Divine revelation in the entire creation in all things, and look upon the writings of the Saints; then we see, find, and grasp the true ground; for in John 1:1-3 it is written: *In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and God was the Word; the same was in the beginning with God. All things were made through it, and without it was nothing made that was made.*

# Chapter 2. The Election of Grace. 15

8. The will of the <term>Abyss: original German *Ungrund*; refers to the "Groundless" or the nothingness before God manifests</term> moves toward a Foundation, as toward a Divine grasping, where the will focuses itself into a *Center* to form a foundation—the Essence of God—and leads itself into Power, and out of that Power proceeds as Spirit. Within that Spirit, it models itself into the sensitivity of the Powers; thus these same Powers, which all lie within a Single Power, are the original state of the Word. For the single Will grasps itself within the single Power, wherein all hiddenness lies, and breathes itself out through the Power into contemplation; and this same Wisdom or contemplation is the beginning of the eternal Mind, as the gazing upon itself. This is what is meant by: *In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and God was the Word itself.* <note>The author is quoting John 1:1, framing it as a description of God's internal psychological/spiritual process.</note>

9. The Will is the beginning, which is called God the Father; it grasps itself in Power, and is called God the Son; and the *Ens* <term>Being: original Latin *Ens*; the fundamental "stuff" or essence of a thing</term> of the Power is the <term>Science: original German *Scienz*, from Latin *scientia*; used here not as modern laboratory science, but as an active, inner "knowing" or "attraction" that pulls possibilities into reality</term> and the cause of Speaking—the <term>Essence: original Latin *Essenz*</term> or the distinctiveness of the Single Power, the distribution of the Mind, which the Spirit makes distinct through its proceeding out of the Power.

10. Now, however, no Speaking or Resonating could occur, for the Powers all stand in a single Power in great stillness—unless that same single Delight within the Power grasped itself in a Desire, such as a *Science* or a drawing-inward. That is: the free Delight grasps itself into a *Science* of itself, for a formation of the Powers, so that the Powers may enter into a <term>Compaction: a thickening or solidification of spiritual forces</term> toward a sounding resonance. From this, the *sensual* tongue of the five *Senses* arises—as an inward gazing, feeling, hearing, smelling, and tasting—which, however, should not be understood here in a creaturely way <note>Meaning: these are not yet physical ears or noses, but the spiritual templates for them</note>, but only in the manner of the first sensitivity and findability of a *sensual nature*.

11. Thus it is said here: the Word (as the formed Power) was in the beginning with God. Here, two "Beings" are now understood: the unformed Power, which is *In*; and the formed Power, which is called *By* <note>original: "Bey"</note>. For the latter has stepped into the "Something" toward mobility. The *In* is still, but the *By* is grasped: and out of this grasping and *Science* originates <term>Nature</term> and <term>Creature</term>, along with all existence.

12. And here we should open the eyes of our understanding wide, so that we know how to distinguish between God and <term>Nature</term>, and not merely say: "God wills it, God created it."

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the "Ungrund" (the Abyss or Groundless) and how God moves from a state of non-manifestation into manifestation. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page's discussion on how the Divine Will organizes itself into the "Word" and the "Mind," transitioning from a singular, still unity into the active, sensory foundation of all reality.</meta>

# 15. Chapter 2. Election of Grace.

...Will of the <term>Abyss: original German *Ungrund*; the "groundless" or "bottomless" state of God before creation or self-manifestation</term> into the Ground, as into the Divine formation, where the Will grasps itself into a Center for a foundation, as into the Essence of God, and introduces itself into power, and goes out from the power into Spirit, and in the Spirit models itself into the sensitivity of the powers; thus these same powers, which all lie within one single power, are the original state of the <term>Word: original German *WortS*; referring to the creative Logos</term>. For the single Will grasps itself in the single power, wherein all hiddenness lies, and breathes itself out through the power into contemplation; and this same Wisdom or contemplation is the beginning of the eternal Mind, as the looking-back upon itself; this is what is meant by: The Word was in the beginning with God, and was God Himself. <note>A reference to the opening of the Gospel of John: "In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God."</note>

9. The Will is the beginning, which is called God the Father; it grasps itself in power, and is called God the Son; and the <term>Ens: the essential "being" or "substance" of a thing</term> of the power is the <term>Scienz: from Latin *scientia*; Böhme uses this to mean an inner, active knowledge or a magnetic attraction that "knows" how to form itself</term> and cause of the Speaking, as the Essence or the distinctness of the Single Power, as the distribution of the Mind, which the Spirit makes distinct through its outflowing from the power.

10. Now, however, no "out-speaking" or sounding could occur, for the powers all stand in a single power in great stillness; unless that same single delight [Luft] in the power grasped itself into a desire, as into a <term>Scienz</term> or an "in-drawing"; that is, the free delight grasps itself into a <term>Scienz</term> of itself, toward a forming of the powers, so that the powers enter into a compaction toward a sonorous sound, from which the sensual tongue of the 5 <term>Senses: original Latin *Senſuum*</term> arises, as an inward beholding, feeling, hearing, smelling, and tasting—which, however, should not be understood here as "creaturely" <note>meaning: not yet belonging to physical biological beings</note>, but only in the manner of the first sensitivity and findability of a sensual kind.

11. Thus it is then called here: the Word (as the formed power) was in the beginning with God; for here two "beings" are now understood: the unformed power, which is called **In**; and the formed power, which is called **With** [or **By**], for it has stepped into the "Something" toward movability. The **In** is still, but the **With** is grasped; and out of this grasping and <term>Scienz</term> originates Nature and Creature, along with all being.

12. And here we should open the eyes of our understanding wide, so that we know how to distinguish between God and Nature, and not merely say: "God wills, God created."

It explains the origin of the five senses as spiritual principles and distinguishes between God's inner stillness ("In") and His outward manifestation ("With").</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exposition of the second chapter regarding the "Election of Grace." It follows the previous page’s discussion on how the Divine Will organizes itself from a singular, hidden power into the "Speaking Word" or Logos. Here, Böhme warns against purely intellectual speculation and introduces the famous "candle analogy" to explain how the divine light emerges from the "fire" of nature.</meta>

# XV. Regarding Chapter 2.

It is not enough that one plays tricks with the Holy Spirit and calls Him a devil, as the captive reason does when it says: God wills evil. For every evil will is a devil, being a self-conceived will toward "selfhood" <note>German *Eigenheit*; refers to the egoistic separation from the divine whole</note>, a rebel against the whole Essence, and a fantasy.

13. Therefore, I highly exhort the reader to rightly grasp our meaning, and to avoid the fantasy of logical conclusions (without the true inward ground); we wish to present the true ground to him here.

14. Understand: The powers toward the Word are God, and the <term>Scienz: from Latin *scientia*; Böhme uses this to describe an active, magnetic "pulling" or an inner knowledge that initiates the formation of nature</term>, as the magnetic attraction, is the beginning of nature. Now, the powers could not become manifest without this desire of attraction; God’s Majesty, in actual power toward joy and glory, would not be manifest without the pulling of the Desire, and there would also be no light in Divine power if the Desire did not pull itself in and cast a shadow, within which the ground of darkness is understood; this darkness then leads to the igniting of the fire, where God calls Himself an angry God and a consuming fire—where the great distinctness, as well as death and dying, and then the great, manifest, creaturely life originates <note>original: *urständet*</note> and is understood.

15. Just as you have a parable of this in a burning candle: the fire pulls the candle into itself and consumes it; there the essence dies away—that is, in the dying of the darkness, it transmutes <note>original: *transmutiret*</note> itself in the fire into a spirit and into another quality (as is understood in the light). For in the candle, one does not perceive a truly sentient life, but with the igniting of the fire, the <term>Ens: the essential substance or "being" of a thing</term> of the candle introduces itself through consumption into a painful, sentient being and life; out of which painful, sentient life the "Nothing" (as the One) becomes shining and light in a great chamber.

16. Thus we must also think of God: that He introduces His will into a <term>Scienz</term> toward nature so that His power may be manifest in light and majesty, and become a kingdom of joy. For if no nature arose within the eternal One, everything would be still; but nature introduces itself into <term>painfulness: German *Peinlichkeit*; used here to mean the labor, distress, or sharp tension necessary for existence</term>, sensitivity, and "findability" <note>German *Findlichkeit*; the state of being able to be found or perceived</note>, so that the eternal stillness may become active, and the powers toward the Word may become audible. Not that the Eternal becomes painful because of this, (so

<meta>catchword: little</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains that God uses the "friction" of nature and a magnetic "Desire" (Scienz) to manifest His hidden light, using the analogy of a candle being consumed by fire to produce light. He argues that without the "painful" tension of nature, the divine joy and power would remain a silent, unmanifested void.</summary>

...any more than the light becomes pained by the fire) but rather that the fiery property, in its painfulness, might stir the silent delight.

17. Nature is the tool of the silent eternity, so that it may form, create, and distinguish, and contain itself therein within a kingdom of joy; for the eternal will reveals its Word through nature. The Word takes on nature within the <term>Scienz: derived from the Latin *scientia* (knowledge); Böhme uses this to describe an active, magnetic "pulling" or an inner knowledge that initiates the formation of something out of nothing</term>; but the eternal One, as the GOD JEHOVAH, takes on no nature Himself, but rather dwells through nature—just as the sun dwells in the elements, or as the "Nothing" dwells in the light of the fire; for the fire’s luster makes the Nothing visible, even though one should not call it a "Nothing." For this Nothing is both GOD and All; we only speak in this way so that we might give our meaning and concept to the reader <note>Böhme often refers to God as the *Ungrund* or "Nothing" to signify that He is beyond all created things—a "no-thing" that precedes existence</note>.

18. Nature, with its <term>original state: *Urstand*; the primordial foundation or source from which a thing arises</term> within the Scienz—as in the attractive desire—is understood as follows: I wish to present a parable of the fire and the light, so that the reader might, with the assistance of Divine power, enter into the true meaning and understanding.

19. Look at a lit candle, and you see a parable of both the Divine and the natural essence. In the candle, everything lies mixed together in one essence, in equal weight and without distinction: such as the tallow, the fire, the light, the air, the water, and the earth. Likewise, the <term>Sulphur</term>, the <term>Mercury</term>, the <term>Salt</term>, and the Oil <note>These are the "Three Principles" of alchemy (Salt, Sulphur, Mercury) which Böhme uses to describe the spiritual makeup of all reality</note>, from which the fire, light, air, and water originate. In the candle itself, one cannot maintain any distinction and say: "this is fire," "this is light," "this is air," or "this is earthly." One sees no cause of the sulphur, salt, or oil; one says it is simply tallow, and that is true. Yet all these properties lie within it, though without any distinction of recognition, for they all stand in equal weight, in the <term>Temperatur: temperament or balance; a state where all opposing qualities are harmonized so that no single one dominates</term>.

20. In the same way, we are to recognize the eternal One as the hidden, unmanifest God, outside of the eternal Scienz—that is, outside of the powerful revelation of His Word. All powers and properties lie within the beginningless GOD JEHOVAH in the Temperatur. But as the eternal Will (which is the Father of all beings and of all original states) contains itself within Wisdom, in a <term>mind: *Gemüthe*; the seat of the soul's inner disposition and creative will</term>, toward its own seat and toward power, and as it breathes out that same containment, so His will contains itself in the...

### XV. Regarding Chapter 2.

...out-breathing of His power, in the temperament in the going-forth of Himself, into a <term>Science: Böhme uses the word "Scienz" (from the Latin *scientia*) to mean an active, magnetic "pulling" or a spiritual focus that allows the formless to take shape</term> toward <term>differentiation: *Schiedlichkeit*; the state of being distinct, divisible, or separable</term>, and toward the revelation of powers; so that in the One, an infinite multiplicity of powers might appear as an eternal glimpse, in order that the eternal One might be distinguishable, sensible, visible, tangible, and substantial.

21. And in this same Science, or in-drawing desire (as one might call it for the sake of understanding), the eternal Nature begins, and within Nature, the being; understand this as a spiritual being, as the <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"</note> Great Mystery, as the manifest GOD (or as one might put it, the Divine Revelation). Here, Holy Scripture speaks of GOD in various ways: as, "GOD is good"; also, "GOD is angry and jealous"; also, "GOD can will nothing evil"; also, "GOD hardens their hearts, so that they do not believe and are not saved"; also, "There is, or there happens, no evil in the city that the LORD does not do"; also, "For this reason I have raised you up, that I might show the power of my wrath in you." Also, the entire choice between good and evil, and everything of which the Scripture speaks; including the great distinction between evil and good in creation, such as evil and good creatures; likewise, to be seen in metals, earths, stones, herbs, trees, and elements—all of this has its beginning and <term>original state: *Urstand*; the primordial foundation or source from which a thing arises</term> from this source.

22. And in Nature, one thing is always set against another, so that one is the enemy of the other—and yet not for the purpose of being enemies, but rather so that one might stir the other in strife and reveal itself, in order that the Great Mystery might enter into differentiation, and that in the eternal One there might be a <term>vivid stirring: *Erheblichkeit*; a quality of being raised up or made manifest and joyful</term> and a kingdom of joy. This is so that the "Nothing" <note>Böhme refers to God as "Nothing" because He is beyond all created "things"</note> may have something to work and play with, like the Spirit of GOD, which has led itself from eternity through Wisdom into such a spiritual Mystery for its own contemplation. He has also led this Mystery into a beginning for Creation and for time, capturing it within the essence and weaving of the four elements, making the invisible spiritual world visible with and within time.

23. And we show you a true image of this in the world: in the sun, stars, and elements, and in the Mystery from which the four elements originate. We see that the sun shines in the depths of the world, and its rays ignite the <term>essence: *Ens*; a Latin term for being or the fundamental substance of a thing</term> of the earth, from which everything grows: also

## Chapter 2. The Election of Grace.

...we understand that it ignites the <term>Ens: being or essential substance</term> within the <term>Great Mystery: *Mysterium Magnum*; the chaotic or potential state from which all things arise</term> as if in the <term>Spirit of the World: *Spiritus Mundi*; the life-force or soul animating the physical universe</term> (namely in <term>Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt: the three alchemical principles representing the soul, spirit, and body of all matter</term>), in which the magical fire is opened up, from which the air, the water, and the earth take their <term>original state: *Urstand*; primordial foundation</term>. That is: the single element in the Great Mystery of the external world then divides itself into four elements, which were certainly present in the Mystery before, but they exist within the "Science" <note>Science here refers to *Scienz*, Böhme's term for an active, magnetic "knowing" or "pulling" that gives form to spirit</note>, in the magnetic <term>impression: the process by which spiritual desire "compresses" into substance</term> into one another, hidden within the Great Mystery, and lying within a single Being.

24. Now, just as the sun’s power and rays unlock the Mystery of the external world so that creatures and plants grow out of it; so also, conversely, the Mystery of the external world is a cause in which the sun’s rays unlock and ignite themselves. If the Great Mystery—consisting of Sulfur, Salt, and Mercury in a spiritual kind and quality—did not lie within the Spirit of the World as the "Science" of the stars' qualities (which is a <term>Quintessence: *Quinta Essentia*; the purest fifth element that transcends the other four</term> over the four elements), then the sun's rays could not become manifest. Because the sun is nobler and one degree deeper in nature than the Mystery of the external world (that is, the Spirit of the World in Sulfur, Salt, and Mercury, in the Quintessence of the stars), it penetrates into the external Mystery and ignites it, and thereby also ignites itself, so that its rays become fiery; for otherwise they would not be fiery without the Science in the Mystery of this world.

25. And just as the sun now introduces its desire violently into the Science, into the Mystery, as into these three first principles—namely, Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt—to ignite and reveal itself in them: so also the Science leads its desire out of the Quintessence of the stars, through these three first principles, just as violently toward the sun, as toward its **Nature-God**, which is a soul of the Great Mystery in the external elemental world, serving as a likeness of the inner, hidden God.

26. One also sees how the stars are so greedy and hungry for the sun’s power that they introduce their Science and desire, in a magnetic fashion within the Spirit of the World, into the three first principles, and draw the sun’s power into themselves; conversely, the sun also powerfully penetrates into them to receive their Science: for this reason, they [receive] their shine from the sun's power... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword for the next page is "have."</note>

# XV. From Chapter 2.

...have, so that they in turn cast their ignited power, as a fruit, into the four elements, and thus qualify within one another; each is the revelation, as well as the power and life of the other, and also the breaking of the other, so that one property does not rise above all the others.

27. Thus the Highest spoke it into a likeness according to his own being, from his eternally speaking Word, out of the eternal great Mystery <note>original: *Mysterio*; referring to the *Mysterium Magnum*, the chaotic potential from which all creation emerges</note> (which is entirely spiritual) into a time, representing the eternal within time through a figure. In this figure, all creaturely life <term>originates: *urständet*; literally "takes its primordial stand"</term>, and conducts its governance there; with the exception of the angels and eternal spirits, as well as the true inner soul of the genuine human. These have their origin from the eternal, beginningless Science <note>original: *Scienz*; in Böhme's vocabulary, this refers to an active, magnetic "knowing" or desire that precedes physical nature</note> or nature, as shall be reported hereafter.

28. Now understand this proposed likeness: **God** is the eternal sun, as the eternal single Good. However, He would not be manifest with His solar-power as **Majesty** <note>original: *Majestät*</note> outside of the eternal Science <note>original: *Scienz*</note> (as the eternal nature) without the eternal spiritual nature. For there would be nothing outside of nature in which **God** could be manifest in His power; for He is the beginning of nature, yet He does not lead Himself out of the eternal One into an eternal beginning toward nature because He wishes to be something evil. Rather, He does this so that His power may enter into **Majesty**, as if into distinction and sensitivity, and so that there might be a movement and a "play" within Him. In this play, the powers play with one another, and through their love-play and wrestling, they reveal, find, and feel themselves. From this, the great, immeasurable love-fire is active within the bond and the birth of the **Holy Trinity** <note>original: *H. Dreyfaltigkeit*</note>.

29. We give you a further likeness of this in fire and light: the fire, in its painfulness <note>Böhme uses "painfulness" (*Peinlichkeit*) to describe the raw, consuming quality of energy before it is transformed into light</note>, points us toward nature within the Science <note>original: *Scienz*</note>, and the light points us toward the Divine love-fire. For light is also a fire, but a giving fire; it gives itself into all things, and in its giving, there is life and being—namely air and a spiritual water. In this soulful water, the love-fire of the light conducts its life, for it is the food of the light. Otherwise, if the light were to be imprisoned, and the spiritual water could not separate itself from the fiery quality, and were to resolve itself into itself with the Nothing, as with the Groundless <note>original: *Ungrunde*; the "Unground" or abyss of absolute potential that precedes even God’s self-manifestation</note>, then the [light] would extinguish...

...light: as it resolves itself with the Groundless <note>original: *Ungrunde*; Böhme’s term for the absolute abyss or void that precedes all created things</note> (within which the eternal ground nevertheless lies), as if in a Temperament <note>original: *Temperatur*; a state of perfect harmony where all conflicting qualities are balanced</note>, where all powers lie together as one; so the Light-fire or Love-fire draws that same spiritual water (which in this resolution becomes more of an oil or Tincture than a power of fire and light-shining) back into itself for its nourishment.

30. And herein lies the greatest Secret <term>Arcanum: a hidden, spiritual mystery or alchemical secret</term> of how to eat spiritually. Dear sons, if you knew this, you would have the ground of all secrecy, and the Essence of all essences; and of this Christ told us: He would give us water of eternal life, which would well up in us into a fountain of eternal life, John 4:14. Not the external water from the external light-fire, but the internal, born of the Divine light-fire, of which the external is but an image.

31. For the eternal Will, which is called Father, leads his Heart or Son, as his power, out through the fire into a great triumph of the Kingdom of Joy.

32. In the fire is death: For the eternal Nothing dies in the fire, and out of that dying comes the holy life; not that it is a literal dying, but rather that the Love-life <term>takes its primordial origin: *urständet*</term> out of the anguish <note>original: *Peinlichkeit*; refers to the stinging, restless quality of the early stages of nature's formation</note>. Thus the Nothing or the Unity takes an eternal life into itself so that it might be sentient, yet it goes out from the fire again as a "nothing"—just as we see that light shines out from fire, and yet remains like a "nothing," being only a lovely, giving, and active power.

33. Therefore understand (in the separation of the Science, where fire and light divide) that eternal nature is found within the fire: Therein **God** says that He is an angry, jealous **God**, and a consuming fire; this aspect is not called the "holy **God**," but rather His zeal—a consuming force of whatever the desire grasps into differentiation within the Science.

There, each property desires to taste the other in great fiery love, and everything is just one completely lovely, mutually <term>in-qualifying</term> <note>original: *inqualirende*; Böhme’s term for properties that work together, influence, and permeate one another</note> power; yet, through the differentiation of the **Science**, they introduce themselves into manifold colors, powers, and virtues, for the revelation of the infinite Divine Wisdom.

36. ### Chapter 2. The Election of Grace. 23

...contain within themselves, because of their original state <note>original: *Urstand*; the primordial condition of a thing</note>, since in the **Fifth Essence** <note>original: *Quinta Essentia*; the purest, most spiritual form of matter</note> the Temperament <note>The state of perfect balance between all divine qualities</note> still lies within, and also at the end shall be separated.

37. And it should be correctly understood in this place; that in the Divine Power—insofar as **God** is called **God**, as in the Word of the Divine properties—no will toward evil can exist, nor is any knowledge of evil within it. Rather, the knowledge of Good and Evil exists only where the bottomless Will separates itself into the fiery **Science** <term>Scienz: a spiritual "knowing" or magnetic longing that acts as a creative force</term>, wherein the natural and creaturely ground lies.

38. For no creature can exist or be born from the Divine Love-**Science** alone; rather, it must have the fiery Triangle of the fiery **Science**, according to the "painfulness" <note>original: *Peinlichkeit*; the state of being driven or pricked into existence</note>, within itself. This acts as a self-will, which goes forth as a particle—like a breathed-out **Science** and a ray from the whole Will—out of the Temperament of the first bottomless Will, where the Word of powers separates in the fire, and out of the fire back into the light.

39. There, the angels and the soul of man have their origin, as if from the fiery **Science** of the beginning of eternal Nature; there, that same ray of fiery **Science** should again incorporate itself into the Light-Temperament, as into the Whole. In this way, it eats from the holy Tincture <note>A spiritualizing essence or "dye" that transforms what it touches</note> of the fire and the light, namely from the spiritual water, in which the fire becomes a Kingdom of Joy.

40. For the Spirit-water is a daily dying-away <note>original: *Ertödtung*; the process of mortifying or "killing off" the harsh aspects of the self</note> of the fiery **Science**, through which the fiery **Science** becomes a Temperament with the Love-fire; then there is only a Single Will within it, namely, to love everything that stands in this root. This is how it should be understood regarding the angels of **God** and also the blessed souls, who all have their origin from the fire's **Science**, in which **Science** the light of **God** shines. Thus they have a constant hunger for Divine power and love, and they introduce holy love into their fire as food, through which the fiery Triangle is transformed into pure holiness and love, and into great joy. For nothing is or exists eternally unless it has its origin from the Eternal, beginningless Will, out of the fiery **Science** of the Word of **God**, as shall be reported hereafter.

<sig>B4</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains that the human soul originates in a "fiery" state of longing that must be tempered by divine light and "Spirit-water" to achieve a state of holy balance and love. He argues that evil does not exist in God's essence but arises when the individual will separates from this divine harmony.</summary>
